### Satan's Invisible
# **WORLD**
## DISCOVERED;

-> OR, <-

A choice collection of modern accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note>, proving clearly against the Sadducees <note>The Sadducees were a Jewish sect from the time of Jesus who were famously skeptical of the supernatural, specifically denying the resurrection and the existence of spirits. Here, the term is used to describe contemporary skeptics.</note> and atheists of this present age that there are devils, spirits, witches, and apparitions, drawn from authentic records, the testimony of famous witnesses, and undoubted truth.

-> To all of which is added, <-
-> The marvelous history of Major Weir <note>Thomas Weir was a highly respected Scottish Covenanter who shocked Edinburgh in 1670 by confessing to a secret life of witchcraft and incest; he and his sister Jane were subsequently executed.</note> <-
-> and his sister: <-
-> With two accounts of apparitions at <-
-> Edinburgh. <-

---

-> By Mr. George Sinclair <note>original: "Sinclar"</note>, former Professor of Philosophy <-
-> at the College of Glasgow. <-

---

> No man should be so conceited <note>original: "vain"</note> as to think he can diminish the merit of a book; for the lowliest scoundrel <note>original: "meanest Rogue"</note> may burn a city or kill a hero, whereas he could never build the one or equal the other. —Sir George Mackenzie <note>original: "Sr. G. McK." Mackenzie was a prominent Scottish lawyer and author known as "Bluidy Mackenzie" for his persecution of Covenanters.</note>

---

-> Edinburgh, Printed by John Reid. 1685. <-

<summary>This is the title page for George Sinclair's 1685 treatise, a foundational Scottish text that uses contemporary "true" stories of ghosts and witches to argue for the reality of the spirit world. It notably features the infamous case of Major Weir, a pillar of the community turned confessed warlock.</summary>

*Whatever imperfection is in it, I make up for it with the sincerity of my respect as the one who presents it. It is a subject that the most learned prince and greatest monarch of his time <note>A reference to King James VI & I, who wrote "Daemonologie" in 1597.</note> was not ashamed to treat. I earnestly implore your Lordship's protection, for I have to deal with many adversaries of this* **Visible World**, *who will not fail to attack me <note>original: "ride tilts at me"</note> with* **Bulrushes** *and* **withered grass.** <note>original: "Windle-strawes" — thin, dry stalks of grass, implying weak or flimsy arguments.</note> *As for those of* **Satan's Invisible World**, *in the Name of the* **LORD**, *I defy them. I know the sharpness of your Lordship's intellect <note>original: "Ingine" — from the Latin 'ingenium,' meaning natural ability or wit.</note> and the depth of your knowledge in all such and other matters, so that there is no theme which I am able to discuss, or thesis which I am able to publish, that your Lordship is not able to protect by your power and authority, and defend by your reason.*

*I am all the more encouraged to offer this to your Lordship,* **A Noble Patrician**, *because I have seen treatises on this subject dedicated <note>original: "consecrated"</note> to* **Kings, Dukes, and Princes.** *I have certain experience of your Lordship's generosity and favorable support, which gave wings to my* **New Philosophical Experiments** <note>Sinclair's earlier scientific work, "Hydrostaticks" (1672), which dealt with the weight of water and air.</note> *to fly through* **Holland, France,**

If my writings had such good fortune and success abroad, through the favorable <note>original: "auspicious"</note> influence of your Lordship’s name upon the title page <note>original: "Frontispiece"</note>, what must they have at home, where your power, influence, and connections <note>original: "Relations"</note> are so significant? Here, your ancestors are so famous, having been useful to their country in peace and war; some of them were employed in public affairs at home and abroad, and all of them were most eminent for their unstained loyalty to their Prince, and zeal for his interests. This lineage extends from the time of Malcolm III <note>Malcolm Canmore, King of Scotland (reigned 1058–1093).</note>, six hundred years ago, to your Lordship, who is the twenty-fifth in direct line <note>original: "Lineally descended"</note> from Dougald, the first of the family.

Christian <note>Christian Bruce, sister of King Robert the Bruce, who married Sir Christopher Seton.</note> was given to him in marriage, and the Fleur-de-lis <note>original: "Flower-de-Luce"</note> was added to his coat of arms—one of the chief ornaments of the Royal Badge.

->A grandfather mighty in war brings fame to you,<-

-><note>original: "Patruo te Principe celsum, Bellipotens illustrat Avus." — A quote from the poet Claudian, emphasizing that the subject's nobility comes from both his warring ancestors and his high-ranking relatives.</note><-

Was not Alexander Seton—the second son of Lord William and Lady Katherine Sinclair (or rather, Saint Katherine, who built the famous Chapel of Seton)—made head of the noble family of Gordons because of his excellent qualities of body and mind, by marrying the heiress <note>original: "Heretrix"</note> of Huntly? And was that not three hundred years ago? And was not his sister Isabel, by the same mother, given in marriage to that most princely youth John Stuart of Darnley? From those two, the royal line <note>original: "Royal Race"</note> is descended. So, I may say without flattery that your family is descended from princes, and likewise, princes are descended from your family.

---

->What blood is more venerable, what origin greater,<-

<header>Dedicatory.</header>
<sig>* 4</sig>

[Three hundred and] fifty years ago, <note>Sir Alexander Seton was appointed Governor of Berwick in 1333; the author is likely writing around 1683.</note> he was made Governor of *Berwick*. The *King of England* <note>Edward III.</note>, while he was besieging the town, could neither by threats nor fair promises entice or persuade him to betray his trust. The garrison suffering from a great shortage of provisions, a truce was made on this condition: that if relief did not come before a certain limited day, the town should be surrendered. As the *Scottish Army* approached under the leadership of the valiant *Douglas*, and *King Edward* feared that the town might be relieved, he sent word to the *Governor*. He informed him (even though the days of the truce had not yet expired) that if he did not surrender the town immediately, both of his sons—one a *hostage*, the other a *prisoner of war*—would be executed <note>original: "crucified"</note> immediately before his eyes.

But the magnanimous *father* and incomparable *mother* <note>Lady Christian Bruce, sister of King Robert the Bruce.</note> being driven to this unsolvable dilemma:

->Scylla besieges the right side, and the relentless Charybdis the left.<-
-><note>original: "Dextrum Scylla latus, lævum implacata Charybdis Obsidet." — A line from Virgil’s Aeneid describing the two sea monsters of Greek mythology. It symbolizes being trapped between two equally perilous choices.</note><-

Love for their dearest children *persuading* them,

<meta>This page continues the description of the moral courage shown by Sir Alexander Seton and his wife during the Siege of Berwick, followed by a tribute to Lord George Seton.</meta>

...and love and respect for their country and family dissuading them, they carry themselves like two invincible **heroes**. By admirable **resolution** and **constancy**—beyond what **Roman histories** can parallel—they prefer their country to their dearest pledges <note>Pledges: Refers to their sons, who were held as hostages and prisoners.</note>, fidelity to treachery, and glory to shame. Regarding this barbarity, the famous poet **John Johnston of Aberdeen** <note>original: "Johannes Johnstonus Aberdonenſis"</note> writes as follows in his book entitled *The Scottish Heroes*:

> Turn your eyes away from here, and do not look back upon the sad fates of your own people... Love of country and constant virtue in the breast conquers all, being strong enough to endure everything for the fatherland.
> <note>original Latin: "Huc averte oculos, neu tristia fata tuorum, Respice -------- Vincit amor Patriæ constansque in pectore virtus, Omnia pro Patria sustinuisse valens."</note>

This strange, tyrannical, barbarous, and monstrous deed <note>original: "Fact"</note> is suppressed in the *Histories of England* and buried in silence—and not unwisely, as it is capable of neither **defense** nor **excuse**.

->My Lord,<-

Neither can I pass over in silence **Lord George**, the fifth of that name:

# Dedicatory.

Your great-great-grandfather <note>original: "Great Grand-Fathers Father"</note>, who *being Master of the Royal Household <note>original: "Oeconomy"</note> to the Queen, was sent by order of Parliament to France to arrange a marriage between* Francis the Dauphin <note>original: "Dolphin"</note> *and* Mary, Queen of Scots. *And afterward, he was sent by King James, her son, to Henry III to confirm the ancient alliance. His third son,* Alexander, *because of his knowledge and skill in jurisprudence <note>original: "Juris-Pru-dence"</note>, was for many years a Senator of the College of Justice, and thereafter President of the Senate, and one of the King's honorable Privy Counselors, and at last was made Earl of Dunfermline <note>original: "Dumfermling"</note> and Lord High Chancellor of* SCOTLAND *by King James. From him, through his two daughters, he had two grandchildren, two matchless heroes:* John, Duke of Lauderdale, *and* John, Earl of Tweeddale. *Both of them, as was said of* Julius Caesar *and* Cato, *were men of* **great virtue** <note>original: "Ingenti Virtute"</note>, *and most eminent talents and endowments.*

> Brave men are born from the brave and good;
> The virtue of the fathers exists in the steers
> And in the horses; nor do fierce
> Eagles give birth to the timid dove.
> <note>original Latin: "Fortes creantur fortibus, & bonis Est in Juvencis, est in equis patrum Virtus: nec imbellem feroces Progenerant aquilæ columbam." This is a quote from the Roman poet Horace.</note>

# The Epistle

*I come at last to your* **Lordship’s father** *and* **grandfather**, *who, imitating their* **noble ancestors**, *were notable examples of* **loyalty** <note>original: "Love"</note> *and* **devotion** <note>original: "Piety"</note> *towards their* **sovereign**; *both were men of great* **humility** *and* **goodness**. *Your* **grandfather** *was admirable, even to excess, for his* **generosity** <note>original: "Liberalitie"</note> *to his other children, and* **hospitality** *to friends and strangers, in a manner more becoming a prince than a subject. Your* **mother**, *of the* **renowned Gordons**, *was a paragon of* **nature** *for her matchless* **beauty** *and* **goodness,**

->**A face of rare beauty, Dido most lovely in form.**<-
-><note>original Latin: "Rara quidem facie, forma pulcherrima Dido." A reference to Queen Dido from Virgil's Aeneid.</note><-

**My Lord,**

*I have touched briefly upon the* **history** *of your* **family**, *but cannot recount all that it deserves. It is well done by others, and expanded by the* **learned notes** *of* **Sir Alexander Seton of Pitmeden, Knight Baronet**, *that honorable gentleman, and one of the honorable* **senators** *of the* **College of Justice** <note>The supreme civil court of Scotland.</note>.

*But for what purpose, my Lord, have I celebrated the praises of your* **predecessors**? *Is it because I have nothing to say of* **yourself**? *Far be it. Your large* **possessions**, *purchased by the*

The prudence and heroic valor of your forefathers are managed with frugality and moderation.

->It is no less a virtue to keep what is won than to win it:<-
->chance is in the winning, but keeping it is a work of skill.<-
-><note>original Latin: "Non minor est virtus quam quærere, parta tueri: Casus inest illic, hic erit artis opus." From Ovid's *Ars Amatoria*.</note><-

The ancient honor and dignity of your family is preserved without the least stain or spot of disloyalty; so that I may truly say, as is engraved upon the front <note>original: "Frontispiece"</note> of your stately palace, **You add honor to ancestral honor.** <note>original Latin: "Decus decori addis avito."</note>

This treatise is titled **Satan’s Invisible World Discovered**, but I am certain that by your exceptional skill, you have discovered an "invisible world" far beyond anything your ancestors achieved. I mean your underground <note>original: "subterraneous"</note> world—a work fit for a prince, and a subject for a great philosopher like Athanasius Kircher to write about. The windings <note>original: "Meanders"</note> and twists <note>original: "Boutgates," a Scots term for a circuitous path</note> found within it are to be admired rather than believed. Even Daedalus <note>The legendary Greek architect who designed the Labyrinth of Minos.</note>, for all his skill, would lose his way there! What digging of mines and levels! What piercing of passages <note>original: "Gaes," a technical term in old Scottish mining referring to channels or veins in the rock.</note>! What cutting through impenetrable rocks, done with more difficulty than Han—

<header>The Epistle</header>

## Hannibal cut through the Alps.

->Who broke the mountains with vinegar.<-
<note>original Latin: "Qui montes rupit aceto." A reference to the Roman satirist Juvenal, describing the legend that Hannibal used heat and vinegar to crack the rocks of the Alps to create a passage for his army.</note>

What **deep pits** and **air holes** are dug! What diligence is used to prevent **damps** <term>choke-damp or fire-damp: toxic or explosive gases that naturally accumulate in mines</term>, which kill men and beasts in a moment! What clever design of **pillars** for supporting houses and churches that are undermined! What floods of **water** run through the **labyrinths** for several miles through a free **level** <term>a horizontal tunnel designed to drain water from a mine by gravity</term>, as if they were conducted by a **guide**! How **Art** and **Nature** strive together to see which of the two shall advance your **Lordship’s interest** most! What ingenious **mechanical engines** has your **Lordship**, like another **Archimedes** <note>The ancient Greek mathematician and inventor known for his complex engineering and machines.</note>, designed for your **coal works** and for draining **coal sinks** <term>shafts or pits where water collects in a mine</term>! What a **massive rampart** <note>original: "moliminous Rampier." *Moliminous* means massive or achieved with great effort; *rampier* refers here to a breakwater or pier.</note> your **Lordship** has begun and nearly perfected to create a **harbor** of deep water, even at **neap tides**!

->A harbor, shielded from the reach of winds, and vast.<-
<note>original Latin: "Portus ab accessu ventorum immotus & ingens." A line from Virgil's *Aeneid* describing a safe haven.</note>

How bountiful has **Nature** been in forming a choice **coal** underground, within a stone's throw of your **Newhaven** <note>This refers to the harbor at Port Seton, which the Earl of Winton significantly improved or rebuilt during this period.</note>?

<meta>The catchword "Your" indicates the start of the next page: "Your Lordship’s ancestor..."</meta>

### Dedicatory.

Your practical skill in improving your coal for the production of salt is praiseworthy. Your defense of the salt pans <term>large, shallow vessels used to evaporate seawater to produce salt</term> against the commanding waves of the raging sea from the northeast is extraordinary. Your splitting <note>original: "renting." In this context, it means tearing or breaking apart rocks, likely using early explosives or heavy manual labor.</note> of rocks to clear passages into your harbors—a feat that none of your ancestors were able to achieve—is amazing.

As the result of the wise management of your affairs benefits you personally, so it also contributes to the public advantage of the country and others, so that men may say:

---
-> To believe himself born for the whole nation <-
<note>original Latin: "Te toti genitum se credere genti." A variation of a line by the Roman poet Lucan, used to describe a person who lives for the benefit of society rather than just for themselves.</note>
---

How many hundreds of young and old people find their existence and livelihood through their dependence on your Lordship's virtuous actions regarding the coal and salt industries and their related works! You are the greatest coal and salt master in Scotland; a nobleman—and the greatest nobleman in Scotland—who is a coal and salt master. Indeed, you are absolutely the most skilled in both trades of all men in the nation. What fruitful grain fields, where Ceres <term>the Roman goddess of agriculture and grain</term> has her chief home, lie within the view of your house at Seton? Which

<meta>This page concludes George Sinclar's dedication to the Earl of Winton, transitioning from the Earl's agricultural and industrial wealth to a summary of his personal character and a final plea for patronage.</meta>

persuades me to maintain this paradox: there is no subject in Britain who has as much incidental <note>original: "casual." In 17th-century finance, this referred to irregular or accidental income, such as fines, royalties, or windfalls, rather than fixed rents.</note> and land-revenue within a mile of his house as your Lordship has.

In a word, your friendliness in conversation, your moderation in diet and clothing, your friendship and kindness to your friends, and your honesty and intellect <term>original: "ingenuity." In this era, it often meant high-mindedness, nobility of character, or cleverness.</term>, combined with the prudent management of your affairs, have endeared all men to you. So I may say that if your ancestors were famous of old for their feats of war during wartime, so is your Lordship famous for your arts of peace during peacetime.

*But, my Lord, I fear I am becoming tiresome, and therefore I shall close as I began, asking for your patronage <note>original: "patrocination."</note> of this small handbook <term>original: "enchiridion." A Greek word for a concise manual or book intended to be kept "at hand."</term>. And as I have long since been devoted to you in all duty and love, so shall I fervently pray for your preservation and happiness both in this life and the next, as long as I live; and I shall consider myself happy to be known as your Lordship’s,*

->Most Dutiful and<-
->Obedient Servant,<-

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page contains a Latin poem of praise (an encomium) dedicated to George Sinclar and his work. It transitions from Sinclar's previous academic and theological writings to his current investigation into the supernatural and the demonic realm.</meta>

<page-type>preface</page-type>
# To the Author and his Work, a Poem of Praise <note>original: "In Auctorem & Opus, Encomiasticon." An encomium is a formal expression of high praise.</note>

We marvel at the many monuments to your genius, George,
And at the bright fame your writings have earned throughout the
->world<- <note>original: "orbem"</note>
As we see so many learned works—the fruit of nightly study—
Issued year after year from your hand. You refute false
Doctrines, revealing the mysteries of the divine Word,
And you show the right path through such great dangers,
So that a lover of sacred truth may travel
Safely, his mind strengthened by holy light,
Never straying from the path of truth.
Now you attempt a different work, opening the
Halls of the kingdom of Hell <note>original: "Tartarei"</note>, and you teach with wise
Counsel what kind of power has been permitted by God—
Who holds supreme authority over all things—to the Devil <note>original: "diti" - referring to Dis, the Roman god of the underworld, here used for Satan</note>.
You show his great wickedness, the horrors he prepares,
And how he constantly threatens destruction to the human race;
You show the deaths he has cruelly caused,
And the wretched souls he has sent down to the grave <note>original: "orco" - Orcus, a name for the underworld or the god of the dead</note>.
You also relate strange but true accounts of apparitions <note>original: "simulachra"</note>
Seen throughout cities, wandering everywhere in the darkness of night—
A horror and a sin even to speak of. You tell of souls roused
From their graves who filled familiar homes with lamentations,
Confessing crimes from their lives that no one knew:
The dark arts of sorcery <note>original: "Medeas artes" - referring to Medea, the famous sorceress of Greek mythology</note>, or how they made pacts
Of dreadful servitude with the tyrant of the Styx <note>original: "stygio tyranno" - i.e., the Devil</note>,
Or committed murder or even worse crimes. You do not use
Examples sought from far away, but those which recent times
Have produced, and which we have even witnessed in our own lands.
Who, then, could doubt that there are ghosts <note>original: "manes"</note> and subterranean kingdoms,
And flames, and a Stygian lake thick with sulfur?
Or doubt those souls whom nature has forbidden to die?
When they read these wonders, they will see that a time is coming
When all spirits shall finally return to their familiar bodies
And receive the rewards for the lives they lived.
Therefore, let men be mindful of these things, while the opportunity remains—

<meta>catchword: While (original: "Dum")</meta>

<meta>This page concludes the Latin poem of praise for Sinclair and provides a note to the reader regarding the philosophical sources (specifically critics of Cartesianism) that support the author's arguments against certain modern "absurd" ideas.</meta>

While reason and time allow <note>original Latin: "Dum ratio tempusque sinuat," literally meaning while time winds or unfolds</note>, and you breathe the heavenly air, hasten your pace; seek peace from the venerable Divine Power <note>original: "Numinis"</note> and ask for forgiveness for your deeds. You also, Sinclair, continue as you have begun to serve the cause of true religion; worthy praise will never be lacking for your pious endeavors, and future fame will celebrate your labors.

->**Patrick Sinclair**<-

Reader, if you question what I have said in the Preface concerning <note>original: "anent"</note> the number of these absurd tenets maintained by the Cartesian <note>Followers of the philosopher René Descartes, whose "rationalist" approach was often seen by traditionalists as leading to skepticism or heresy.</note> philosophers abroad, I refer you, for full satisfaction, to Petrus van Mastricht, that famous Professor of Theology at Utrecht. In his recent book titled *The Gangrene of Cartesianism* <note>original: "Gangræna Cartesianismi"</note>, he has listed them all, and far more than I have mentioned, citing the author, book, and page, and has notably refuted <note>original: "confuted"</note> them. For proof of what I have written concerning those other tenets mentioned on the last page of the book, see Malebranche's books, *The Search after Truth* <note>original: "La Recherche de la Verite"</note>, *On Nature and Grace* <note>original: "De la Nature, & de la Grace"</note>, and his *Christian Meditations* <note>original: "Meditations Chrestiens"</note>, together with Mr. Arnauld's book *On True and False Ideas* <note>original: "de Vraes, & de Fausses Idees"</note>, where all these opinions are ridiculed and most rationally refuted.

### THE
# PREFACE
### To The
## READER

My purpose is simply to use a few collected accounts to prove the existence of *devils*, *spirits*, *witches*, and *apparitions*. The *philosophical arguments* used for this purpose, though very convincing, are often so deep and theoretical that they require more focus and insight than many learned men—who are not used to such contemplation—are willing to give. Nor can the common and average reader understand such *reasoning*. Therefore, I believe people are best convinced by evidence that appeals to the senses, such as the following *narratives* <note>original: "Relations"</note>, which leave a deeper and more lasting impression on the mind than thousands of subtle *metaphysical arguments*.

Considered on its own, this *essay* is modest and of little importance. However, when viewed as a defense of the *outer works of religion* <note>The author views belief in spirits as a protective "outer wall" that supports the central truths of Christianity.</note>—which the bold and reckless lack of faith of some has attacked—it will be considered timely. This is especially true now, while *atheism* and *Quakerism*—that "sink of folly and madness" (as one writer calls it) from which it is a short leap into the other—are so prevalent. While various factions vent their grudges against one another, and men scramble for their own *opinions* <note>original: "Conceits"</note> and private advantages, they fail to see how this damnable evil—a lesson never believed even in hell, for *there are no atheists in hell* <note>original: *Nullus in Inferno Atheus*</note>—is gaining ground.

<summary>The author explains that he has compiled stories of the supernatural because physical evidence of spirits is more persuasive to ordinary people than abstract philosophy. He positions his book as a defense against the rising "evils" of atheism and Quakerism.</summary>
<keywords>demonology, atheism, Quakerism, metaphysics, skepticism, religious defense, 17th-century theology</keywords>

<meta>The author continues his preface, transitioning from the general threat of skepticism to the specific sources and reliability of the supernatural accounts he has compiled. Catchword: published.</meta>

# The Preface.

Atheism exists now, as it did before <note>original: *Atheisme est, ante fuit*</note>; it comes on with large strides and enters the breach which the skeptics have made. If this prevails, farewell to all *religion*, all *faith*, and all hope of a life to come. 

> *Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we must die.* 

Sober and wise men have often said that if they did not believe they would live again, they would not desire to live for a moment.

The *accounts* <note>original: "Relations"</note> are plain and easy to understand, and all of them can be verified by *authentic records* or by famous witnesses. There are no "old wives' tales" <note>original: "old Wives prattles about the fire"</note> here, but only those that can withstand the *test* and strict trial of any man’s examination. Just as much belief as is given to any human histories and matters of *fact* related by famous writers may be given to these following accounts. 

I have collected some of them from *Saducismus Triumphatus* <note>original: "Sadducism Triumphed Over," a famous 1681 book by Joseph Glanvill and Henry More defending the existence of spirits.</note>, that excellent book composed by *Doctor Glanvil* and *Doctor More*, and the rest are acquired from people of eminent honesty and faith. This book is not the worse, but rather the better, because I have transcribed some of the best accounts from there and inserted them here. It is impossible to write a book of this nature without the author collecting stories, since it depends not on a man's own imagination but essentially upon information from others. 

The advantage here is that a man may own this book for a small price, even if it contained no other *accounts* than those found in the other volume. Meanwhile, that other book cannot be sold in shops for less than six or seven shillings. There is one *account* here—specifically, the *Devil of Glenluce*—which I took great pains to research. *Doctor More* valued this story so much that he copied it word-for-word from my *Hydrostatics* <note>original: *Hydrostatica*, a 1672 scientific work by the author, George Sinclair, which included the supernatural story of the Glenluce weaver.</note> and thought it worth his while to include it among his own *accounts*. It is now enlarged by many excellent additions and

<sig>A 2</sig>

published here again. No one yet, whom I have ever heard of, has had the nerve to say that it was merely a *trick* and an *imposture* to amuse and strike wonder into simple and gullible people. I believe if the most *hardened atheist* <note>original: "Obduredest Atheist"</note> among men would seriously and in good earnest consider that *account* <note>original: "Relation"</note> and ponder all its circumstances, he would immediately cry out, as a *Doctor* of *Medicine* <note>original: "Physick"</note> did upon hearing a less significant story: *"If this is true, I have been on the wrong track all this time; I must begin my calculations anew."* This single *account* is worth the entire price of the book. 

Some have indeed also said that the *Demon of Tedworth* <note>A famous 1662 haunting case in Wiltshire, England, involving a ghostly drummer; Joseph Glanvill wrote extensively about it.</note> was only an imposture, and that *Doctor Glanvil* himself admitted as much, but he has sufficiently vindicated the truth; men only speak as they wish things to be. Many would be glad if all such *accounts* were acknowledged to be nothing but *tricks* and *pranks* <note>original: "Waggeries"</note> so that they might live however they please. This is because men are deathly afraid to believe that there is any such thing as a *spirit*, lest there should also be a *devil* and a judgment after this life. They are intolerant of anything that implies it, so that they may indulge their lusts with full abandon and without any fear of a future reckoning, living like *animals* <note>original: "Brutes"</note> and dying the same way. 

I have also included a notable story of the devil that troubled the house of a *Protestant Minister* at *Mâcon* in *France* <note>The "Devil of Mâcon" (1612) was a famous poltergeist case involving the minister François Perrault.</note> to show that no one can claim protection from his malice. The *Devil* tries harder to strike the *shepherd* than any of the *flock*, though he is a total enemy to both. Many worthy *ministers* of the *Gospel* have been the *target* <note>original: "Butt"</note> of the *devil's* malice, an example of which is clear from the twenty-first *account*.

But what are the reasons why there is so much disbelief in *devils, witches*, and *apparitions*? There

<header>The Preface</header>

There is, first, an affected tendency <note>original: "humour"</note> in many to joke, scoff, and mock all such accounts <note>original: "Relations"</note>; they are more willing to believe in a *World in the Moon* than in the truth of such a narrative. Because witchcraft is a broad subject to expound upon, men find ample opportunity to speak of frauds and impostors, of juggling tricks, of depressive episodes of unbalanced people, and of *old wives' tales*. They talk of these extensively, and so conclude—through their playful and frivolous imagination—that whatever is said by sober men concerning the reality of witches and spirits is merely ridiculous and inconsistent with reason. 

To this end, the mocking wit <note>original: "Drolling Wagge"</note>, stirring up and heightening his mocking mood—especially with a glass of good claret—quips cleverly. By making others laugh, they think of him (and he thinks of himself) as *a third Cato fallen from heaven* <note>original: *tertium Cato de caelo cecidit*; a satirical reference to the Roman poet Juvenal, describing a person who poses as a profound moral authority</note>. In reality, he is just a man like his neighbors. Although he only intended to be frivolous at first, through such frequent joking (just as a man who lies as a sport eventually makes a habit of lying in earnest), his reason becomes an obedient slave to his imagination. He eventually concludes in seriousness that there are neither devils nor witches. 

His half-witted listeners admire him and take every jest for an argument. They view his loud laughs at an idle tale of a devil or a witch as proof that no such creatures exist. The other reason why people disbelieve in witches and spirits is atheism. For if one pays close attention, he will find it lurking at the root of the Sadducean principle <note>The Sadducees were a Jewish sect in the time of Jesus who famously denied the existence of spirits, angels, and the resurrection of the dead.</note>. It is probable that the Sadducees in Christ’s time were as great defenders and advocates of "witches" as either Reginald Scot the Englishman—the *Father of the Witch-Defenders* <note>original: "Patrons"</note> whom King James mentions in his *Demonology*—or John Webster, John Wagstaffe, or the author of the *Anonymous Pamphlet* <note>original: "Namle Pamphlet"</note> printed in the year 1659 <note>original: "Anno 1659"</note>.

<summary>The author argues that skepticism toward the supernatural arises from a combination of mocking intellectual vanity and underlying atheism, specifically comparing modern skeptics to the ancient Sadducees.</summary>

<meta>The author continues to argue against skeptics of witchcraft, having previously addressed the roles of mockery and atheism in their disbelief. He now moves to a third argument regarding the validity of confessions and the historical/biblical evidence for witchcraft.</meta>

A third reason is that many commonly believe that many innocent people have suffered as *witches*, especially those who have been *tortured* into a confession. Even if that were true, does it follow that everyone suffers in that same way? And although many of their confessions seem ridiculous—such as their *transformation into cats and hares* or their *transportation to far countries*, which is obvious enough—other things cannot be considered ridiculous. These include renouncing their *baptism*, giving themselves up from *head to toe* <note>original: "top to toe"</note> to the *Devil’s service*, receiving his *mark* (which they take willingly), and accepting commands to perform all kinds of evil. 

If we believe that the Devil can speak with an audible voice and appear in a visible shape (as he likely did when appearing to our Savior), why should it be considered unbelievable that he could do the same to ordinary men and women? Satan offered to make a clear bargain with *Christ*. He said, "*If you will fall down and worship me, I will give you all these kingdoms*." Why then—even more so—might he not make compacts with men and women? 

If *witchcraft* were merely a new trade that had never been heard of before, and if only a few people in a single region claimed skill in it and told such foolish stories about themselves, then a person might be considered odd <note>original: "singular"</note> for believing them. But since this practice has been confirmed by millions in all ages and all places, and has received the general agreement of all sober, sensible men—and is especially well-confirmed by Holy Scripture—why should anyone, such as *Webster* and *Wagstaff* <note>John Webster and John Wagstaffe were 17th-century English authors known for their skeptical works challenging the reality of witchcraft and spirits.</note>, judge all witchcraft to be nothing but delusions? *Samuel said to Saul that rebellion was like the sin of witchcraft; that is, the one is as great a sin as the other* <note>referencing the biblical verse 1 Samuel 15:23</note>. Would the Spirit of God ever compare *rebellion*, especially rebellion against Himself, to a

...to a non-entity, to a thing that does not exist, or to *juggling tricks*, or *sleight of hand* <note>original: "Legerdemain"</note>, such as *stage magicians* <note>original: "Hocus Pocusses"</note> perform. Were *Jannes* and *Jambres*, the *Egyptian magicians* who opposed *Moses*, merely two *jugglers* or *swindling rogues* who threw down *carved and painted serpents* before *Pharaoh* instead of real ones? They must have been very skillfully made for true *serpents* to mistake them for real ones! It would have been a victory worth bragging about indeed that *Moses* won over *Pharaoh’s magicians* if they were nothing but *absolute cheats* who had no more to do with evil spirits (or evil spirits with them) than evil spirits have to do with any other sinners. 

There is also that well-known passage, *Exodus 22:18*: "*You shall not allow a witch to live*." This would be a law of extreme severity, or rather cruelty, if it were directed against a poor *juggler* for his *sleight of hand* tricks. Why is *witchcraft* called a "*work of the flesh*" and counted among the rest of those damnable sins in *Galatians 5*? The Greek word used there is *Pharmakeia*—meaning *poisoning* <note>original Latin: "veneficium"</note>, *sorcery*, or the *art of poisoning*—an art in which they are highly skilled thanks to their master, that ancient *druggist* <note>The author likely refers to the Devil as a master of herbal and chemical poisons.</note>. 

They do not usually harm people with natural poisons like *arsenic* or similar substances, but rather through a hellish malignancy infused into objects by the Devil’s skill and malice. It is a poison for which no *antidote* <note>original Greek: "Alexipharmacon"</note> or *herbal remedy* <note>original: "Orvieton," a famous 17th-century panacea or antidote.</note> can serve as a cure. Men and women have been harmed by the touch of a *witch’s* hand, or by the breath and kiss of their mouth, as has been well known recently. They also harm by their looks, which is called *physical fascination* <note>original Latin: "Fascinatio physica"</note>, a *fore-look* <note>The "evil eye."</note>, or *evil eyes*. 

The Greek word is *Bascania*, from the verb *Bascaino*, meaning to bewitch or envy—*as if killing with the eyes* <note>original Greek and Latin: "quasi phasei kaino, oculis interficio"</note>. This occurs when a *witch* sends venomous and poisonous spirits from her heart through her eyes, like *rays*, which kill a man when they strike him. In the same way, the *basilisk* <note>A mythical serpent-like creature said to kill with its gaze.</note> kills...

...kills in this way. But the Devil and the **basilisk** <note>A mythical reptile mentioned in the Bible, often associated with a deadly gaze or breath; here, it symbolizes the Devil's poisonous nature.</note> are both **serpents**. Who was **Simon Magus**? <note>A figure in the New Testament (Acts 8) who practiced sorcery and attempted to buy the power of the Holy Spirit.</note> And especially consider that notable **magician Apollonius of Tyana** <note>original: "Apollonius Thianeus." A 1st-century Greek philosopher and teacher who was often compared to Jesus and accused of being a sorcerer.</note>, one who went beyond almost anyone the world ever knew. There was also that young woman possessed with a spirit of divination: in the Greek, it is a **spirit of Python** <note>original: "Spirit of Python." In Greek mythology, Python was the dragon/serpent guarding the Delphic oracle; the term came to mean a "ventriloquist" or a spirit of prophecy.</note>, the name by which **Apollo** (the Devil) was called by the heathens.

The **Witch of Endor** <note>A biblical woman (1 Samuel 28) who summoned the spirit of the prophet Samuel at the request of King Saul.</note> is such a notable and evident instance of an alliance <note>original: "Confederacy"</note> with the Devil that the defenders of witches <note>original: "Witch Patrons." Writers like Reginald Scot who argued that "witches" were either frauds or victims of mental illness.</note>, in their attempt to find an excuse, have strained their wits to the limit. But it is such a **choke-pear** <note>A "choke-pear" is a metaphor for a harsh, unanswerable argument or a fact that is impossible to accept.</note> to them that they shall never be able to chew or swallow it. **Doctor Glanvill** <note>Joseph Glanvill (1636–1680), an English philosopher and clergyman who defended the reality of witchcraft in his book *Saducismus Triumphatus*.</note> maintains that it was the true **Samuel** who appeared to **Saul**. But it is far more probable that it was the Devil in **Samuel's** likeness. 

First, it is most incredible that God—who had just then refused to answer **Saul** by the means He Himself had appointed—would answer him, or allow **Samuel** to answer him, through the use of those occult means which God detested. Secondly, if it had been the true **Samuel**, or some **good spirit**, he would not have accepted that worship from **Saul**, which good spirits would not allow (Revelation 19:10 and 22:8, 9). Thirdly, among the other sins for which he condemns **Saul**, he omits this one: asking counsel from someone who had a **familiar spirit** <note>A demon or supernatural entity believed to assist a witch.</note> to inquire of it. Yet for this transgression, along with others, he is expressly said to have died (1 Chronicles 10:13). The true **Samuel**, who was so zealous for God’s honor and so faithful a rebuker of sin, would never have neglected to mention this, especially since he caught **Saul** in the very act. 

Lastly, the spirit claims to be disquieted and brought up by **Saul's** urging and the witch’s art. This is a most false, impious, and absurd thing to imagine concerning those blessed souls who have returned to their...

# The Preface

...God; Eccl. 12:7, Isaiah 57:2, Luke 16:22, Revelation 13:14.

In every age, many have written about sorcery and witchcraft—men of eminent knowledge and talent. We can see this in the example of King James's *Demonology* <note>Published in 1597, King James VI and I wrote this philosophical dissertation on contemporary necromancy and the methods used by demons to influence the world.</note>, a work as far superior to all other men’s writings on that subject as he himself was superior to all other princes of his time. I cannot fail to mention the opinion of his Majesty’s current Advocate, Sir George Mackenzie—a gentleman of great knowledge and experience in all such matters. In his 16th Pleading for Mevia <note>A legal case involving a woman named Mevia who was accused of witchcraft; Mackenzie used the case to discuss the intersection of law and the supernatural.</note>, who was accused of witchcraft, he says: "I do not share the opinion of those who deny the existence of witches, though I think they are not numerous; and I believe that some are permitted by Providence so that the existence of spirits may not be denied, etc." And in his work *Criminal Law* <note>original: "Criminals." Referring to Mackenzie's "Laws and Customs of Scotland in Matters Criminal" (1678), a highly influential legal textbook.</note>, he acts as a theologian, a lawyer, and an insightful philosopher regarding the nature of witchcraft.

But what is the cause of so much atheism in the world? There are many reasons, but I shall only touch upon two. First, there is a monstrous rabble of men who, following the principles of Thomas Hobbes and Baruch Spinoza, slight religion and undervalue the Scriptures. They do this because the Bible makes such express mention of spirits and angels, which their dull and leaden <note>original: "plumbeous." Meaning heavy or lead-like; used here to describe an intellect that cannot grasp spiritual concepts.</note> intellects cannot conceive. Consequently, they believe that everything in the universe falls under the category of material things and physical bodies only; therefore, they conclude there is no God, no Devil, no spirit, and no witch. Hobbes the Englishman is too well known for his atheistic writings. Benedictus Spinoza—or rather *Maledictus* <note>A pun on the philosopher's name: "Benedictus" means "blessed" in Latin, while "Maledictus" means "cursed."</note>—is a foreigner and a profane abuser of the Scriptures. He claims that all those demons which Christ and his disciples cast out were merely...

<summary>The author cites King James and Sir George Mackenzie as authorities on the reality of witchcraft while blaming the rise of atheism on the materialist philosophies of Hobbes and Spinoza.</summary>

<keywords>King James, Demonology, Sir George Mackenzie, Baruch Spinoza, Thomas Hobbes, atheism, materialism, witchcraft laws</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>The author is continuing his argument from the previous page, detailing the causes of atheism in the world. Having previously blamed the followers of Hobbes and Spinoza, he now turns his critique toward the followers of René Descartes.</meta>

...be but diseases in men's bodies.

There is a second reason, namely the absurd principles of **Cartesian Philosophy** <note>The philosophical system of René Descartes (1596–1650), which emphasized reason and systematic doubt as the foundation of knowledge.</note>, which are eagerly maintained by Descartes's followers in their public writings and debates abroad. They do not indeed assert that there is no God; rather, they seem to prove His existence, especially by the "Idea" of Him which is inherent to all men. This may seem like a plausible argument, but when it is put to the test or touchstone, it—along with others of that kind—is not found sufficient, nor able to convince atheists. To show that their principles are absurd and dangerous, I shall mention a few of them which are publicly owned and maintained abroad, especially in **Holland** <note>The Dutch Republic was a major center for intellectual debate and the publication of Cartesian and Spinozist works in the 17th century.</note>.

1. We must doubt everything before we can come to any clear or distinct knowledge. We must suppose (they say) there is no God, no **heavens**, no **earth**; and even—something a man ought to tremble to speak of—that God is a deceiver <note>Descartes used the "deceiving God" or "evil demon" hypothesis as a radical thought experiment to see if anything could be known with absolute certainty.</note>.
2. They encroach upon sound, **orthodox religion** and the **Articles of our Faith**, going quite contrary to all **Christian** and **Protestant theologians**; they would have **orthodox theology** reformed by their fanatical **philosophy**.
3. They claim that **philosophy** is not subservient to, or a "handmaid" of, theology <note>original: "Hand-maid to Divinity." Traditionally, philosophy was seen as a tool to support religious doctrine ("ancilla theologiae").</note>.
4. They claim that **philosophy** is as certain as divine truth, and is as much a revelation as the **Scriptures** are.
5. They argue that the **Scriptures**, in **natural** matters, speak according to the erroneous opinions of the **common people** <note>original: "the Vulgar." This refers to the "theory of accommodation," the idea that God adapted the language of the Bible to the limited scientific understanding of its original audience.</note>. They believe that **philosophy** and **philosophers** are the true **interpreters** of the Scriptures in natural matters.
6. They argue that the **Scriptures**, in **moral and practical** matters, also speak according to the erroneous judgment and opinion of the **common people**.
7. They argue that the **Scriptures**, in matters of **faith**, speak also according to the erroneous opinion of the **common people**.
8. They claim that **philosophy** is the infallible interpreter...

# The Preface

(48) That "Personal Subsistence"—namely, that existence by which the human nature exists—is nothing but a mere fiction <note>original: "figment"</note> of theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note>. (54) That death is not the separation of the soul and body. (55) That the resurrection is not the reunion of the soul and the body. (56) That the souls of the righteous do not properly pass immediately into heaven. (57) That the souls of the wicked do not immediately pass into hell. 

These are a part of the *Cartesian tenets* <note>The doctrines of René Descartes and his followers.</note>. What dangerous principles they are, and what dreadful consequences may be drawn from them, is evident to all sober men. *James Arminius* <note>A Dutch theologian whose views led to the "Arminian" challenge against strict Calvinism.</note> and his followers, called *Remonstrants*, caused much trouble to the *Belgian* <note>original: "Belgick"</note> *churches*, but it is not comparable to what this man *Descartes* <note>original: "Cartes"</note>, and especially his followers, have done. The *Arminian tenets* were only five in number regarding their main points and had some shadow of reason. But the *Cartesian novelties* far exceed the number of heads which the *Hydra* or *Lernean Serpent* <note>A mythical multi-headed monster that grew two heads back for every one cut off.</note> had. They are pregnant with and ready to give birth to *Vaninianism*, *Arianism*, *Socinianism*, *Vorstianism*, and *Nestorianism* <note>These are various theological heresies. Vaninianism (atheistic materialism), Arianism and Socinianism (denial of the Trinity/divinity of Christ), Vorstianism (errors regarding God's nature), and Nestorianism (dividing the person of Christ).</note>. *Cartesian novelties* (says a learned man) *far exceed the Arminian ones by many leagues* <note>original: "Novitates Cartesianae multis parasangis superant Arminianas"</note>; the *Cartesian novelties* go far beyond the *Arminian tenets* in both number and weight.

# To the Reader.

They not only *gnaw about* <note>original: *arrodunt*</note> as a *gangrene*, but they *eat up* <note>original: *exedunt*</note> the most part of the whole body of *theology*, to the utter ruin of the *Christian faith*. Let no man mistake me, for it is not my design to discourage any man who teaches or learns the *new philosophy* <note>A reference to the "Scientific Revolution" and the new systems of thought, like those of Descartes, emerging in the 17th century.</note>. *I myself love the new philosophy, truly not because it is new, but because it is good* <note>original: *Ego ipse amo novam Philosophiam, verum non quia est nova, sed quia est bona.*</note>. It is very fitting that *students in philosophy* should have *a little of everything* <note>original: *ex omni aliquid*</note>—they should know all the different *sects* and opinions of learned men for their personal accomplishment—provided that first, as the *Great Philosopher* <note>Usually a reference to Aristotle.</note> says, this axiom be observed: *Whatever is moved, is moved by something at rest* <note>original: *Quicquid movetur aliquo quiescente movetur.*</note>. There ought to be laid down some sure principles as a foundation upon which *students* may walk safely; principles which, because of their evidence and certainty, may never be called into question. *Archimedes* spoke well when someone asked him if he was able to move the Earth with his mechanical engines: "*Give me a place where I shall stand, and I shall move the earth*" <note>original: *da ubi stabo, & terram movebo.*</note>. "Give me a place to stand upon," says he, "and I shall move the *Earth*," implying that no design, no matter how small or how great, can be accomplished without sure grounds laid down first as a foundation. Otherwise, men might resolve to build *castles in the air*.

To put an end to this matter, I shall ask the *defenders of witchcraft* <note>original: "Witch Patrons"</note> why *Pharaoh’s magicians* were not able, by their *enchantments*, to turn the *dust of Egypt* into *lice*, as *Aaron* did with his *staff* <note>original: "Rod"</note>. They threw down their counterfeit *serpents*, turned water into blood, and brought up *frogs* upon the *land of Egypt*; and yet they were not able to produce *lice*, the lowliest <note>original: "meanest"</note> of all *vermin*. It is said the *magicians* did so with their *enchantments*—that is, they endeavored by their *magical art* to do it, but could not. For, they said to *Pharaoh*, "*The finger of God is in this*"; for neither we nor

If the second point is argued—namely, that the Lord only stopped these *jugglers* <note>In the 17th century, "jugglers" referred to any performers using deception or tricks, similar to modern street magicians or con artists.</note> from using simple *sleight of hand*—then consider the consequences. She had been there for four weeks before, but she seldom visited Sir George Maxwell’s house until at length she became more familiar and spoke with his two daughters. The following day, she told the young women that this woman (whose name was Jennet Mathie, the widow <note>original: relict</note> of John Stewart, an assistant miller <note>original: under-Miller</note> at Shaw Mill <note>original: Schaw-Mill</note>) had made a wax figure <note>original: Wax-Picture</note> with pins in its side. But a bystander declared that he was no longer on *Pollok* land, but was in *Darnley*. To which *Sir George* replied, "I hope my fingers may reach him in *Darnley*."

This was the only thing that could be considered a provocation toward *Mathie*. In the meantime, no harm was done to her son, whom *Sir George* to this hour does not even know by sight <note>original: by the face</note>, but has allowed him to live in his mother’s house throughout the entire period of his sickness, even since her imprisonment. Meanwhile, as *Mathie* remained obstinate, she was searched for insensible marks <note>Witch-marks, or areas on the skin believed to be insensitive to pain, thought to be signs of a pact with the Devil.</note> before the *Sheriff Depute* of *Renfrew* and many notable <note>original: famous</note> witnesses at *Paisley*; very many such marks were found on her.

Before the court, she claimed she had freed one foot from the hole and used the other to pull the stocks. This was considered completely impossible, as the stocks were extremely heavy and she should not have been able to remove her foot from the hole.*

## Of Satan’s Invisible World <note>This text is part of George Sinclair's famous work "Satan's Invisible World Discovered" (1685), which collected accounts of the supernatural.</note>

## ACCOUNT II.

->Being an Amazing Discovery of a Murder by an Apparition.<-

Around the year of our Lord 1632, near Chester-le-Street <note>original: "Chester in the Street"</note>, there lived a man named Walker. He was a farmer <note>original: "yeoman"; a farmer who owned his own land</note> of good social standing and a widower. He had a young relative named Anna Walker who managed his household. She was suspected by the neighbors to be pregnant. One night, as it was getting dark, she left with a man named Mark Sharp, who was a collier <note>original: "Collier"; a coal miner</note> born in Blackburn Hundred in Lancashire. After she left, she was not heard from for a long time, and little to no fuss was made about her disappearance.

During the winter, James Graham, a miller living about two miles from where Walker lived, was alone very late one night in the mill grinding corn. Around twelve or one o'clock in the morning, as he came down the stairs after putting corn into the hopper, he found that despite the mill doors being shut, a woman was standing in the middle of the floor. Her hair was hanging down around her head, and she was covered in blood, with five large wounds <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; the next page likely details the description of these wounds and the ghost's message.</note>

## ACCOUNT III.
### Concerning the Witchcraft of Agnes Simpson.

Although this is from a date too old to match the timeline of our other stories, since it is a true copy of such an authentic record—even if not a recent one—it may perhaps not be wrong to briefly name some of the effects, types, and circumstances of her sorcery. These include her skill in treating diseases, such as the claim that the sickness of William Black was an **elf-shot** <term>elf-shot</term> <note>A traditional belief that sudden pain or illness in humans or cattle was caused by invisible arrows shot by spirits or elves.</note>. She also had the habit of taking the pains and illnesses of sick people onto herself for a time and then transferring <note>original: "translating"</note> them to a third person. She used long scriptural prayers and rhymes containing the main points of Christianity, so that she might seem to have been not so much a **white witch** <note>A person believed to use magic or supernatural powers for good, particularly for healing.</note> as a holy woman. She used such nonsensical rhymes for the instruction of ignorant people and for teaching them to pray, such as these two prayers: the Black and White **Our Father** <note>original: "Pater Noster"</note> in meter [rhyme], in...

### A Proclamation over the Market Cross
### of Edinburgh, at twelve
### o'clock at night.

After King James IV had mustered his army in the Boroughmuir <note>original: "Borrow-Moor"; an area south of the old city of Edinburgh used as a military staging ground.</note>—which was at that time a large, spacious field, and most pleasant and delightful because of the many stately oaks which overshadowed the place—a proclamation was heard at the Market Cross of the town around midnight in the month of July.

### Martha Discovered <note>The OCR reads "Dilcovered," a common typesetting error where a "long s" was misread as an "l."</note>

...for so many years. And furthermore, she continued crying and praying to the Lord as best she could <note>original: "according to her weak measure"</note>.

Yet she continued that day, being Wednesday, and the next day, Thursday, as before until evening at six o'clock. At that time, she sat by the fire preparing <term>dressing</term> the food. Then there came something like a rushing noise in both her ears, with which it was said to her, "Stand, your walking is given to you again." Then she immediately stood up—she who had for so many years crawled <note>original: "crept"</note>—and went to the door. Her husband, meeting her, was exceedingly afraid and drew back. In the meantime, she cried out, "My dear husband, I can walk again!"

The man, thinking it was a spirit <note>original: "Spirit"; in this context, a ghost or supernatural apparition</note>, drew back, saying, "You are not my wife." But his wife, taking hold of him, said, "My dear husband, I am the selfsame person who has been married to you these thirty-nine years. The Almighty God has made me walk again."

## RELATION VI.

### Strange Pranks Played by the Devil at Woodstock in England, in the Year 1649.

On October 13, 1649, the Commissioners <note>These were Parliamentary officials sent during the English Civil War to survey and seize royal property after the execution of King Charles I.</note> and their servants arrived at the Manor House <note>original: "Marmor-houſe"</note>. They took up their residence in the King’s own rooms: the bedchamber and the withdrawing-room. They turned the former into their kitchen <note>original: "Kitching"</note>, and the Council Hall into their brewhouse. The Presence Chamber <note>original: "Chamber of Preſence"; the room where a monarch traditionally received formal guests</note> served as their meeting place to conduct business, and they made a wood-storage area <note>original: "Wood-houſe"</note> out of the dining room. 

In that dining room, they laid the wood of that ancient tree <note>original: "Standard"</note> from the High Park, known to all by the name of the "King’s Oak." To ensure that nothing remained which bore the name of "King," they had dug the tree up by its roots.

## RELATION VII.

### An Apparition to *King James* IV and his *Courtiers* in the *Church* of *Linlithgow*. <note>original: "Kirk of Lithgow"; Linlithgow is a town in Scotland, home to a royal palace.</note>

*While the king stayed at Linlithgow, waiting for his army to gather—which was later defeated at* Flodden <note>The Battle of Flodden (1513) was a catastrophic defeat for Scotland by the English; James IV became the last monarch from the British Isles to die in battle.</note>—*being full of worry and anxiety, he went into the* church *of Saint Michael to hear* evening song <term>original: "Evening-Song"; a service of evening prayers, known as Vespers or Evensong</term>, *as it was then called. Neither could any of the **bystanders** (of whom several had observed him closely, intending to speak with him afterward) feel or perceive how, when, or where he passed from them, having in a sense vanished from their very midst.

He was so moved that he could eat very little at *dinner*, even though a very fine *feast* had been provided for their farewell to their *friends*. *Indeed*, it was observed that what the *Captain* had seen and heard had a lasting influence on him. Those who were well acquainted with his *character* judged that the *memory* of the event stayed with him, and that the words of his deceased *friend* frequently echoed in his ears for the rest of his life, which lasted about two more years.

# ACCOUNT 9.

## A Remarkable Story of discovered *Witchcraft* at *Lauder* in the year *1649*, and of the *tricks* <note>original: "Wyls" (wiles)</note> which the *Devil* used in deceiving poor *creatures*.

In the beginning of the year 1649, a *common rumor spread among all men and women about an eminent* warlock <term>a male witch</term> *whose name was* Robert Grieve, *also known as* Hob Grieve.

*Grieve* traveled through these parts of the country, deceiving many simple people. He was finally discovered, arrested, and imprisoned in the town of *Lauder*. After long evasions and denials—in which he had learned to be very cunning from his hellish master—by the great goodness of **GOD**, he was eventually brought to a serious acknowledgment of his guilt. He confessed to being the *Devil’s Officer* in that region for more than eighteen years, responsible for summoning all of *Satan’s vassals* <note>original: "Vassals"; those who have pledged service to a lord, in this case, to the Devil</note> to attend meetings whenever and wherever the Devil required. 

He also acknowledged that his *wife* (who had been burnt at *Lauder* about twenty years earlier for witchcraft) was the reason he fell into that snare. Because they were poor and had little or nothing to live on, he began to resent his condition and complain about his lot in life. His *wife*, perceiving this, told him not to be troubled; she showed him that if he would follow her advice, she would introduce him to a *Gentleman* who would teach him a way to become rich. 

He listened to this proposal, and at her request, he went down with her to a haugh <term>a piece of flat land in a river valley</term> on the *Gala Water* <note>original: "Gallow-water"</note>, near the village of *Stow*, where she had arranged to meet the *Gentleman*. Upon hearing this, Mr. Mompesson sent for the man and asked him by what authority he went up and down the country in that manner with his drum. The drummer answered that he had good authority and produced his travel pass <term>Pass</term> <note>An official document during this period that allowed a person—often a soldier or traveler—to move through parishes and sometimes request charity.</note> along with a warrant signed by <note>original: "under the hands of"</note> Sir William Cawley and Colonel Ayliffe of Grittenham <note>original: "Gretenham"</note>.

Mr. Mompesson, being familiar with these gentlemen's handwriting, discovered that the pass and warrant were counterfeit. The man confessed to the fraud <note>original: "Cheat"</note> and begged earnestly to have his drum back. The drumming occurred very frequently, usually for five nights in a row, and then it would stop <note>original: "intermit"</note> for three. The sound was on the outside of the house, which was mostly constructed of wooden boards. It started consistently just as the family was going to sleep, whether it was early or late.

After a month of these disturbances outside, the noise moved into the room where the drum was kept. It occurred four or five nights out of seven, starting within half an hour after they had gone to bed and continuing for nearly two hours. The sign that it was about to begin was a rushing <note>original: "hurling"</note> sound heard in the air over the house. When it ended, there was the beating of a drum, similar to the signal used for the dismissal of a military guard <note>original: "breaking up of a Guard"</note>. 

The noise continued in this room for the space of two months, during which time Mr. Mompesson slept there to observe it. In the early part of the night, it was usually very troublesome, but after two hours, everything would become quiet.

When Mrs. Mompesson was confined to bed for childbirth <note>original: "brought to bed"</note>, there was very little noise on the night she was in labor <note>original: "Travail"</note>, nor was there any for three weeks afterward until she had recovered her strength. But after this polite pause <note>original: "civil Cessation"</note>, the noise returned in a more aggressive <note>original: "ruder"</note> manner than before. It followed and tormented <note>original: "vext"</note> the youngest children, beating their bedsteads with such violence that everyone present expected the beds to fall to pieces. If anyone placed their hands on the beds, they felt no actual blows, but they could perceive the beds to shake...

On the first of November 1662, it made a massive noise. A servant, observing two floorboards in the *children's* room that seemed to be moving, commanded the spirit to give him one of them. The man answered that he knew of none. "No?" said the *Drummer*. "Do you not hear of the drumming at a *gentleman's* house at *Tedworth*?" "I hear enough of that," the other replied.

<meta>This page continues the account of the supernatural disturbances at the Mompesson household in Tedworth, specifically detailing the legal proceedings against the drummer and his alleged connection to the occult.</meta>

"I," said the drummer, "I have plagued him, and he shall never be quiet until he has made satisfaction to me for taking away my drum." Upon this information, the *fellow* was tried as a *witch* at *Salisbury* <note>original: "Sarum"; the historical name for Salisbury</note>. All the main circumstances that I have related were sworn at the *assizes* <note>original: "Assizes"; periodic criminal courts held in England and Wales</note> by the *minister* of the *parish* and various others of the most intelligent and substantial *inhabitants*, who had been eye and ear *witnesses* of them, time after time, for several years together.

The *fellow* was condemned to *transportation* <note>original: "Transportation"; the practice of sending criminals to a penal colony</note> and accordingly sent away. However, I do not know how (it is said by raising storms and frightening the *sailors*), but he managed to return. It is worth noting that during the entire time of his imprisonment and absence, the house was quiet; but as soon as he was at liberty, the *disturbance* returned.

He had been a *soldier* under *Cromwell* and used to talk much of *gallant books* <note>original: "Gallant Books"; likely referring to books on magic or the occult</note>, which he had obtained from an odd *fellow* who was considered a *wizard* <note>original: "Vizzard"; an archaic variant of "wizard"</note>. Having drawn my friend <note>Mr. Hill</note> into another room away from the rest of the company, Compton said he could prove to him that he could do things beyond the ordinary, and asked him whom he desired to see.

**Mr. Hill** *did not have much confidence in his talk*, but named his wife, who was then many miles away from them at her home. Upon this, **Compton** took up a looking-glass <note>a mirror</note> that was in the room and, setting it down again, told my friend to look into it. He did so, and there—as he most solemnly and seriously protested—he saw the exact image of his wife in the clothing <note>original: "habit"</note> which she was then wearing. She was working with her needle in a specific part of the room (which was also represented in the mirror), where he later found she had really been at that very time, according to the inquiries he made when he returned to his own house.

The subject matter of this story is a true and short account of the troubles with which the family of one **Gilbert Campbell**—a weaver by profession in the old parish of **Glenluce** in **Galloway**—was afflicted <note>original: "exercised"</note>. I have ventured to publish it again <note>original: *De Novo*</note> in this book, first because it was previously jumbled together <note>original: "hudled up"</note> among subjects of another nature. But now I have restored it to its own proper place. Next, I include it because this version of the story is more complete, being enlarged with new additions which were not in the former version, and it does not end as abruptly as the other did.

It happened (says my informant, **Gilbert Campbell's** son, who was then a student of philosophy at the College of **Glasgow**) that after one **Alexander Agnew**—a bold and sturdy beggar who was later hanged at **Dumfries** for blasphemy—had threatened to harm the family because he had not received the alms <note>charity</note> he demanded, the said **Gilbert Campbell** was often hindered in his work <note>original: "exercise of his calling"</note>, and yet he could not understand by what means this was being done. ## ACCOUNT XIII.
### King Duff, the 78th King of Scotland, Bewitched.

Although this is well known to all who read our Scottish histories, it will not be out of place to include it here, as it belongs in this collection, especially for the sake of those who have not heard of it. 

While the King was occupied with settling the country and punishing those who disturbed the peace, he began to be severely afflicted in his body with a new and unheard-of disease, with no cause for his sickness appearing in the least. At length, after several remedies and cures were used to no purpose, a report spread—though the authors of the rumor were uncertain—that the King was brought to that sickness and trouble by witches. 

This suspicion arose from an unusual sweating he was under; his body was wasting and withering away little by little, and his strength failed day by day. And since all his physicians had done their utmost, and yet

<summary>This page introduces a historical account of King Duff of Scotland, who suffered from a mysterious wasting disease attributed to witchcraft after medical treatments failed.</summary>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>A World Discovered.</header>

*Yet, with no appearance of recovery, it was supposed his case was extraordinary. Therefore, while everyone was intently focused on the* outcome, *news came to Court that* night meetings *were being held at* Forres, *a town in* Moray <note>original: "Murray"</note>, *for the purpose of taking away the life of the* King. *This was immediately received and believed as the* truth, *because no other explanation occurred at the time that seemed more probable. ## RELATION XIV.
### The Apparition of Edward Avon to his 
### Son-in-Law Thomas Goddard.

Thomas Goddard of Marlborough in the County of Wiltshire <note>original: "Wilts"</note>, a weaver, was traveling to Ogbourne <note>original: "Ogburn"</note> on the ninth of November 1674. At a stile <term>stile: a set of steps or rungs used to help people climb over a fence or wall</term> on the highway around nine in the morning, he met the apparition of his father-in-law, Edward Avon of this town, a glover <term>glover: a maker or seller of gloves</term> who died this past May. The ghost appeared to be wearing the same clothes, hat, stockings, and shoes he usually wore when he was alive, and was standing by and leaning over that stile. 

When Goddard came near, the apparition spoke to him in an audible voice, saying these words: *“Are you afraid?”* 

To which he answered, *“I am, for I am thinking of someone who is dead and buried, whom you resemble.”* 

The apparition replied in the same voice, *“I am he you were thinking of; I am Edward Avon, your father-in-law. Come near to me; I will do you no harm.”* 

To which Goddard answered, *“I trust in Him who bought my soul with His precious blood; you shall do me—”*

# ACCOUNT XV. <note>original: "Relation"</note>

## Some noteworthy examples <note>original: "observable Passages"</note> of God’s providence to a godly minister, in giving him full clarity <note>original: "clearness"</note> concerning **Bessie Graham**, who was suspected of witchcraft.

She was arrested <note>original: "apprehended"</note> around the end of August 1649, following some threatening words she had spoken while drunk to **John Rankin’s** wife in **Kilwinning**. Because of this, the poor woman fell ill ten days later and died shortly thereafter. She was imprisoned in the church tower <term>Steeple: the tower or spire of a church, often used as a temporary jail in Scottish witch trials</term> for thirteen weeks. During that entire time, I (the minister) visited <note>original: "repared"</note> her, but found her to be increasingly hardened <term>obdured: stubborn or unrepentant in the face of persuasion</term>. 

In all her conversations, she was so clever <note>original: "subtile"</note> that I could not gain any advantage through her words; indeed, she sometimes made me think she was an innocent woman. Therefore, I was very distressed by her harsh treatment, if it could have been avoided, and I secretly wished she had never been interfered with <note>original: "medled with"</note>. In fact, if she could have escaped without my involvement, I would have been glad; for I greatly feared that everything we could prove against her would not have

## ACCOUNT XVI.

### Concerning the Apparition of Sir George Villiers.

A few days before the Duke of Buckingham went to Portsmouth—where he was stabbed by Felton <note>John Felton assassinated the Duke in 1628</note>—the ghost of his father, *Sir George Villiers* <note>original: "Williers"</note>, appeared to a man named Parker. Parker was a religious and sober man who had been a servant to the late Sir George and was now a servant to the Duke, his son. The ghost appeared to him in his *morning dressing gown* <note>original: "Morning-Chamber-Gown"</note> and commanded <term>charged: ordered or strictly instructed</term> him to tell his son that he should abandon the *mission* and *plan* he was undertaking, or else he would certainly be murdered. Parker promised the *apparition* that he would do so.

As the Duke continued making preparations for his *expedition*, the *apparition* came to Parker again. It scolded <term>taxing: criticizing or accusing</term> him very severely for breaking his *promise* and required him to delay no longer in informing his son of the danger he was in.

The word *charm*, or *incantation*, comes from the *Latin* word for *song* or *verse* <note>original: "Carmen"</note>, because the *Roman soothsayers* <note>original: "Southsayers"; people who claim to predict the future or use magic</note> delivered their *charms* in *verse*. But words in themselves, whether spoken or written (as these charms are), have no power to bring anything to pass. That power belongs only to *Almighty God*. 

*Charming* is widely practiced by the *Pope* and the *Roman Catholic Church* <note>original: "Romish-Church"</note>. # ACCOUNT XVIII.
<note>original: "RELATION XVIII."</note>

## The Appearance of the Ghost of Mistress Bretton.

Doctor Bretton, the late Rector <term>a member of the clergy in charge of a parish</term> of Ludgate, who lived at Deptford, formerly resided in Herefordshire and married the daughter of Doctor Santer. This gentlewoman was a person of extraordinary piety, which she expressed both during her life and at her death.

She had a maid for whom she had a great kindness; this maid was married to a nearby neighbor, and her name, as I recall, was Alice. Not long after the mistress's death, as Alice was rocking her child in the evening, she was called away from the cradle by someone knocking at the door. When she opened it, she was surprised by the sight of a gentlewoman who could not be distinguished from her late mistress, neither in her person nor her clothing <term>original: "habit"</term>.

She was wearing a morning gown, identical in appearance to the one Alice had often seen her mistress wear. At first sight, Alice expressed very great amazement and said, "If my mistress were not dead, I would have no doubt that you are she." 

The figure replied, "I am the same person who was your mistress," and took Alice by the hand, which Alice affirmed was as cold as a stone.

## Concerning an *Apothecary’s Assistant* <note>original: "Servant"</note> who returned to the Shop after he had died.

*This is a well-known event which happened in the year 1659, at* Crossen <note>Now Krosno Odrzańskie in modern-day Poland.</note> *in* Silesia. *This is a part of* Germany *which long ago was under the* Poles <note>original: "Polonians"</note>, *but is now subject to the Crown of* Bohemia. *The chief magistrate of that town at the time was the* Princess Elizabeth Charlotte, *a person famous in her generation. In the spring of that year, one* Christopher Monig, *a native of* Zerbst <note>original: "Serbest"</note>, *a town belonging to the Princes of* Anhalt, *who was an assistant to an* apothecary, *died and was buried with the usual ceremonies of the* Lutheran Church. *A few days after his death, a figure looking exactly like him in face, clothes, height, demeanor* <note>original: "meen"</note>, *and so on, appeared in the* apothecary’s *shop. There, he would sit down and walk around at times; he would take boxes, pots, and glasses from the shelves and put them back in their places. He would sometimes test and examine the quality* <note>original: "goodness"</note> *of the medicines, and even weigh*

Some people still living today are willing to swear under oath that they clearly remember seeing him after his death. Because the incident was so notorious, a public debate <note>original: "Publick Disputation"</note> was held about it at the University of Leipzig <note>original: "Academy of Leipsig"</note> by a man named Henry Conradus, who debated the matter for his doctoral degree at the university. 

This reminds me of an apothecary at Reichenbach in Silesia about fifteen years ago. After his death, he appeared to several of his acquaintances and cried out that during his lifetime, he had poisoned several men with his drugs. Consequently, the town magistrates <note>original: "Magistrates"</note> took up his body and burned it. After this was done, the spirit disappeared and was seen no more.
