### RELATION XXI.

#### *Regarding Isabel Heriot.*

*This woman was born at Peaston in the Parish of Ormiston and was for several years a very useful servant to the minister there, performing all manner of outdoor work <note>original: "Out-House-Work," referring to labor done in the barns, stables, or fields rather than inside the main residence</note>. She was of a short stature, with a small and slender body and a dark <note>original: "Black," likely referring to a swarthy or tanned skin tone common in historical descriptions of the time</note> complexion. Her head sat somewhat crookedly <note>original: "awry"</note> upon her neck. She had a mocking and jeering sense of humor <note>original: "drolling and jearing humour"</note> and would speak to people of high social standing <note>original: "Persons of Honor"</note> with great confidence.*

*After several years of service, the minister began to dislike her, especially because she did not grow in her knowledge of* **GOD**, *despite having so much opportunity and occasion to learn. Therefore, she was dismissed <note>original: "put away"</note>, and*

### RELATION 24.
Concerning some drunkards destroyed by the Devil.

This has been published in a sermon by a godly minister. However, I must insert it here in its own proper place. On February 8 (says my author), in the year 1578, a company of drunkards, whose names are recorded as follows: Adam Gibbons, George Keepel, John Keyzel, Peter Horsdroff, John Warner, Simon Heamkers, Jacob Hermons, and Hermon Frow. ## RELATION XXV.

### Concerning a Warlock Named *William Barton*.

About thirty years ago, more or less, a man named *William Barton* was arrested for **witchcraft**. His first confession was that if he had twenty sons, he would advise them to avoid the lust of uncleanness. For, he said, "I never saw a beautiful woman, whether a maiden or a wife, without coveting her; this was the only cause that led me to become the **Devil’s servant** <note>original: "Vassal"</note>." 

One day, he said, while traveling from his own house in *Kirkliston* to *Queensferry*, he caught up with a young gentlewoman on **Dalmeny Moor** <note>original: "Muire"</note> who appeared beautiful and graceful. He approached her, but she avoided his company, and when he persisted, she became angry and very **reserved** <note>original: "nyce"; in this context, it means modest, fastidious, or difficult to please</note>. He said, "Since we are both going the same way, please accept my **escort** <term>convey: a companion or escort for a journey</term>." # RELATION 28.

## An Account <note>original: "Relation"</note> of the Strange Witchcraft Discovered in the Village of *Mohra* in *Sweden* <note>original: "Swedeland"</note>.

*The news of this witchcraft reaching the King's ear, His Majesty was pleased to appoint Commissioners—some from the clergy and some from the laity—to make a journey to the aforementioned town to examine the entire matter. Accordingly, the examination was ordered to take place on August 13th. The Commissioners met on the 12th of that month in the said village at the parson's house. Both the minister and several people of standing <note>original: "people of fashion"; meaning respectable or high-ranking citizens</note> complained to them with tears in their eyes about the miserable condition they were in. Therefore, they begged the Commissioners to think of some way by which they might be delivered from that calamity. They gave the Commissioners very strange examples <note>original: "instances"</note> of the Devil's tyranny among them: specifically, how with the help of witches he had lured <note>original: "drawn"</note> some hundreds of children to him and made them subject to his power.*

Secondly, what kind of place it was *they went to, called by them* **Blockula** <note>Blåkulla, a legendary meadow in Swedish folklore where witches were said to gather with the Devil.</note>, *where the* **Witches** *and the* **Devil** *used to meet?* Thirdly, what evil and mischief they *had either done or planned* <note>original: "Designed"</note> *there?*

## First, concerning their journey to Blockula.
### The contents of their confession.

*We of the province of Älvdalen* <note>original: "Elfdale," a district in Dalarna, Sweden</note> *confess that we used to go to a gravel pit which lies close by a crossroad, and there we put on a vest over our heads and then danced in a circle. After this, we ran to the crossroad and called the Devil three times: first with a quiet voice, the second time somewhat louder, and the third time very loud, using these words: "Predecessor* <note>original Latin: "Antecessor," meaning one who goes before or a leader</note>, *come and carry us to Blockula* <note>original: "Blockula," referring to Blåkulla, the legendary island meadow where witches were said to feast with the Devil</note>." *Whereupon he immediately used to appear, but in different clothes* <note>original: "Habits"</note>; *but for the most part, we saw him in a gray coat and red and blue stockings. He had a red beard, a high-crowned hat with linen of various colors wrapped around it, and long garters upon his stockings. It is very remarkable that the Devil never appears to the witches with a sword at his side.*

## The Assembly at Blockula

We come finally to show the mischief and *evil* which the witches promised to do to *men* and *animals* <note>original: "Beasts"</note>. They confessed that they were required to promise the *Devil* that they would do everything that is evil, and that the *Devil* taught them how to *milk* <note>A form of sympathetic magic where a witch was said to magically drain milk from a neighbor's cow from a distance</note>, which was done in this manner: they used to stick a knife in the wall and hang a kind of *strip* <note>original: "Label"</note> on it, which they pulled and stroked. A *woman* confessed that the *Devil* gave her a wooden knife; by taking it into houses, she had the power to kill anything she touched with it. Yet there were few who would confess that they had actually harmed any *man* or *woman*. When asked whether they had murdered any *children*, they confessed that they had indeed tormented many, but they did not know whether any of them had died from those afflictions <note>original: "plagues"</note>.

They added that the *Devil* had shown them several places where he had the power to do mischief.

The *minister* of *Elfdale* <note>Älvdalen, a Swedish parish where the witch hunt was particularly intense</note> declared that one night these witches were, in his estimation, pressing upon the *crown* of his head, and that from that time on, he suffered a long-lasting headache.

In the year <note>original: "ANNO"</note> 1612, a Mr. Perreaud, a Protestant minister in that place, was away from his home one night. While he was gone, his wife, who was in bed, was greatly disturbed by noise and a loud din throughout the house. The next night, she felt something pull the blankets off the bed, and that same night, all the pewter dishes <note>original: "Vessel"</note> and brass candlesticks were flung around the room. 

When the minister returned home, he was told what had happened. He carefully searched every corner of the house before going to bed and secured all the doors and windows to prevent any suspicion of a hoax <note>original: "Imposture"</note>. He had barely gotten into bed when he heard a strange noise in the kitchen, like the rolling of a large iron cannonball <note>original: "Bullet"</note> banging against a wooden paneled wall <note>original: "partition of Wainscot"; wainscot refers to high-quality wood paneling used on interior walls.</note>. At this, the minister went to the room but found nothing.

# ACCOUNT <note>original: "RELATION"</note> XXXII.

## Concerning <note>original: "Anent"</note> Margaret Wilson.

This is a true narrative of what happened to her one night while several people were attending to her. I cannot relate what happened on other nights, as I lack further information. They say that a gentleman, her uncle from the parish of Galashiels, came one Sunday after the sermon to the minister there, Mr. Wilkie, and told him that the Devil was at his house. He said, "There is a strange knocking around the bed where my niece lies." 

The minister went with him and found it to be so. When she got out of bed and sat down to supper, there was such a knocking from below that it terrified everyone present. This knocking occurred directly under her chair, in a place where it was impossible for any mortal person to knock from below. After supper ended, they all went to prayer. When she rose from her seat and went to kneel in another spot, a knocking was heard beneath her there as well, even during the time of prayer. When she was put to bed, with many people attending to her, she fell into a deep sleep. Then her body was

Accordingly, he took her out of the stocks and, embracing her, carried her out of the **prison**. Being terrified by this, she cried out along the way, "*O GOD, where are you taking me!*" At these words, he let her fall at a distance from the steeple about the width of a street in **Edinburgh**, where she broke her legs and her belly. I saw the impression and dimple of her heels, as many thousands did, which remained for six or seven years; no grass would ever grow upon that spot. One person still living there was a witness and can attest to this. The gentleman, seeing the vengeance of *God* upon her for her wickedness, immediately fell to his knees and pleaded with the *Lord* on her behalf; the Lord was graciously pleased to hear him.

<page-num>215</page-num>
<header>A World Discovered.</header>

...to attribute good or evil luck <note>original: "luke"</note> to the flying of birds, as Virgil says, "Often the ill-omened crow has predicted it from the hollow oak." <note>original: *Sæpe sinistra cava prædixit ab ilice Cornix.* From Virgil's *Eclogues*.</note>

Whether there is any magic in the practice of some curious young women who, upon **All Hallows' Eve** <note>Halloween</note>, go to bed without speaking to anyone—having first eaten a cake made of soot—and, while dreaming, see in their sleep the man who shall be their husband, I shall not determine. But it looks like a very bad practice. I heard of a woman who dipped her **smock** <note>A woman's basic undergarment or chemise</note> in **south-running water** <note>Folk traditions often specified that water for charms must be taken from a stream flowing south, against the sun</note> on that night, and hung it up before the fire to dry. Someone comes in, in the likeness of the man who was to be her husband, and turns the garment, then went immediately to the bed where she was awaiting the event, and kissed her. It seems she did not believe it was the **Devil**. 

To speak of the **second sight** <note>The supposed power of seeing future events or things happening elsewhere, traditionally associated with the Scottish Highlands</note> I cannot, until fuller information is given. I am undoubtedly informed that men and women in the **Highlands** can discern fatality approaching others by seeing them in waters or with **winding-sheets** <note>A shroud or cloth used to wrap a dead body for burial</note> about them. And that others can "read" in a **sheep's shoulder bone** <note>A form of divination known as scapulimancy, where the markings on a shoulder blade are interpreted</note> a death within the parish seven or eight days before it comes. It is not improbable that such preternatural knowledge comes first by a **compact with the Devil** and is derived downward <note>Passed down from one generation to another</note>

<page-num>216</page-num>

### Satan’s Invisible World Discovered

By succession to their posterity, many such people, I suppose, are innocent and have this **Sight** <note>The "second sight": the supposed power of seeing future events or spirits</note> against their will and inclination.

Charms and spells were first taught to men and women in league with the Devil; many of these are received by tradition and used by witches and ignorant persons as well. The power of curing must come from the Devil’s active, invisible application of them to a specific disease—such as the curing of a universal gout by this unintelligible charm:

->Etter sheen etter fock, Et ta le-<-
->ur etta pachk Wipper si caan easemitter in-<-
->shi, so leish in shi corne, orn sheip twa till<-
->ane curht mach a mainshore.<-
<note>This text appears to be a phonetic transcription of a nonsensical or corrupted spell, possibly derived from Scots or Gaelic.</note>

There is in some part of **Galloway** <note>A region in southwestern Scotland</note> a charm for curing a disease called the **Ling** <note>An archaic name for a disease, sometimes associated with sheep or cattle, but here used in the context of human folk medicine</note>, using these words: *Cathari Duni Chini Brini.*

There is another charm which some use for achieving that which others do by "casting three knots" <note>A common magical practice of tying knots in a string or ribbon to produce a specific effect, such as curing an illness or binding a lover</note>: *far si far, fa far fay u, far four na forty Kay u Mack straik it a pain four hun creig weil Mack smeoran bun bagie.* This language cannot be interpreted.

Besides this, there are prayers and *Aves* <note>original: "Avies"; refers to the "Ave Maria" or "Hail Mary" prayer</note> among the **Highlanders**, in which they think there...
